Hi, doctor. Thank you so much for seeing me today. Um... well, I guess I'm here because I've been having a little trouble with my eating, if that's okay to talk about? 

It's just... some evenings after work, especially if I've been really good and strict with myself all day, I end up eating... well, quite a bit more than I meant to. Like, I'll have my normal dinner, but then I'll just kind of keep going. I'll have some cereal, maybe some crackers, whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then some sweets... all just within a couple of hours. 

I know it probably just sounds like a silly lack of willpower, and I really hope I'm not wasting your time with this, but... is that the sort of thing you help people with?

Like, I'll have my normal dinner, but then I'll just kind of keep going. I'll have some cereal, maybe some crackers, whatever leftovers are in the fridge, and then some sweets... all just within a couple of hours. \n\nI know it probably just sounds like a silly lack of willpower, and I really hope I'm not wasting your time with this, but... is that the sort of thing you help people with?\n\nDoctor: How do you feel after one of these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well... I mean, I don't feel *great* about it, obviously. I suppose I feel a little silly, mostly? Like, I'm a grown woman, I should be able to just have one bowl of cereal and stop, you know? \n\nIt's just a bit... frustrating, I guess. I try so hard to be good during the day, so when I slip up like that, I just feel a little disappointed in myself. But I don't want to make it sound like some big, dramatic thing. It's just a bad habit I've gotten into, really. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... guilty might be a slightly strong word for it? I mean, I definitely don't feel proud, but I wouldn't say I'm agonizing over it or anything. \n\nIt's more just a little twinge of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have done that,\" and then I just try to move on and promise myself I'll be better the next day. I really try not to dwell on it too much, because I know everyone overindulges sometimes, right? I just... I try to keep a positive attitude about it. Is that... is that a normal way to feel about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for eating more than you meant to, like exercising extra or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yes, I suppose I do try to balance things out a little bit? Just to keep things even, you know? \n\nLike, the next day, if I've had one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it. So I might, um, just skip breakfast and lunch. Or, you know, I like to stay active, so I might take an extra-long hike, or maybe push myself a little harder at softball conditioning... even if I'm feeling a bit tired. \n\nIt just seems like a healthy way to handle it, right? Just a little extra effort to get back on track. I hope that's the right approach? I try really hard to be responsible about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. That sounds... well, it sounds a bit extreme, doesn't it? \n\nI know some people struggle with that sort of thing, and my heart goes out to them, but I just try to stick to the exercise and watching what I eat the next day. I'd never want to do anything that could actually harm my body, you know? I really try to be sensible about my health. That's the right way to look at it, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I'm really thinking about it, for the past few months or so, it's been happening most weeks? Usually just, you know, one or two nights a week where I end up eating a bit more than I planned. \n\nAnd then, like I said, the next day I just try to be really good about it. I'll skip a meal or two, or maybe just stay out on the trail a little longer to make up for it. It's really not every single day or anything out of control. Just a couple of times a week to keep things balanced. That's... that's not too unusual, is it? I feel like a lot of women my age probably do the same thing to stay in shape, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I think my weight has stayed pretty steady, actually. I mean, maybe a pound or two up or down, just normal fluctuation, but nothing significant at all. \n\nI really try to keep on top of it, you know, with the extra walking and just being careful the next day. So it stays pretty much the exact same. I figure as long as my weight isn't jumping all over the place, I'm managing things okay... right? That's usually a good sign?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, afraid? I don't know if I'd use the word afraid. That sounds a little... intense, maybe? \n\nI mean, I definitely prefer not to gain weight, but I think that's just normal, right? Working in sales—I sell insurance, you know—you really want to look presentable and professional for your clients. I just like to maintain where I am so my clothes fit nicely and I feel healthy. I think everyone worries about their waistline a little bit as they get older... wouldn't you say that's pretty standard?\n\nDoctor: How often do you skip meals the day after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it kind of goes hand in hand with those nights I mentioned earlier. So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? Like, a little course correction just once or twice a week?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your exercise routine to be excessive or more than what a typical person your age would do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say it's *excessive*. That sounds like I'm, you know, out of control or something. \n\nI just... like I said, the next day after one of those nights where I eat a bit too much, I just tell myself I need to make up for it. So I might take an extra-long hike, or if we have softball conditioning, I'll just push myself a little harder, even if I'm feeling a bit tired. And I'll usually just skip breakfast and lunch that day, too. \n\nIt's just to keep things balanced, you know? I think a lot of people push themselves a little harder after a big dinner, don't they? It's just being responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to balance out your eating are motivated by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"fear\" is the right word. It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Well, I... I wouldn't say my whole self-worth is tied to it. That makes it sound so shallow, and I really try not to be a shallow person. I know I have value in other ways. 

But... I guess, like I was saying earlier, it does affect how I feel about myself in the moment. Like, if my waistband feels tight before a client luncheon or a leadership meeting, I start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I start worrying that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I've still been doing my job well and my clients trust me. 

It just... it makes me feel less confident, I suppose. Less put-together. But that's just because I want to present myself well professionally, you know? It's important to look the part when you're an agent, right? I'm sure you understand that, being a doctor and having to look professional yourself.

I mean, I definitely don't feel proud, but I wouldn't say I'm agonizing over it or anything. \n\nIt's more just a little twinge of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have done that,\" and then I just try to move on and promise myself I'll be better the next day. I really try not to dwell on it too much, because I know everyone overindulges sometimes, right? I just... I try to keep a positive attitude about it. Is that... is that a normal way to feel about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for eating more than you meant to, like exercising extra or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yes, I suppose I do try to balance things out a little bit? Just to keep things even, you know? \n\nLike, the next day, if I've had one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it. So I might, um, just skip breakfast and lunch. Or, you know, I like to stay active, so I might take an extra-long hike, or maybe push myself a little harder at softball conditioning... even if I'm feeling a bit tired. \n\nIt just seems like a healthy way to handle it, right? Just a little extra effort to get back on track. I hope that's the right approach? I try really hard to be responsible about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. That sounds... well, it sounds a bit extreme, doesn't it? \n\nI know some people struggle with that sort of thing, and my heart goes out to them, but I just try to stick to the exercise and watching what I eat the next day. I'd never want to do anything that could actually harm my body, you know? I really try to be sensible about my health. That's the right way to look at it, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I'm really thinking about it, for the past few months or so, it's been happening most weeks? Usually just, you know, one or two nights a week where I end up eating a bit more than I planned. \n\nAnd then, like I said, the next day I just try to be really good about it. I'll skip a meal or two, or maybe just stay out on the trail a little longer to make up for it. It's really not every single day or anything out of control. Just a couple of times a week to keep things balanced. That's... that's not too unusual, is it? I feel like a lot of women my age probably do the same thing to stay in shape, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I think my weight has stayed pretty steady, actually. I mean, maybe a pound or two up or down, just normal fluctuation, but nothing significant at all. \n\nI really try to keep on top of it, you know, with the extra walking and just being careful the next day. So it stays pretty much the exact same. I figure as long as my weight isn't jumping all over the place, I'm managing things okay... right? That's usually a good sign?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, afraid? I don't know if I'd use the word afraid. That sounds a little... intense, maybe? \n\nI mean, I definitely prefer not to gain weight, but I think that's just normal, right? Working in sales—I sell insurance, you know—you really want to look presentable and professional for your clients. I just like to maintain where I am so my clothes fit nicely and I feel healthy. I think everyone worries about their waistline a little bit as they get older... wouldn't you say that's pretty standard?\n\nDoctor: How often do you skip meals the day after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it kind of goes hand in hand with those nights I mentioned earlier. So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? Like, a little course correction just once or twice a week?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your exercise routine to be excessive or more than what a typical person your age would do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say it's *excessive*. That sounds like I'm, you know, out of control or something. \n\nI just... like I said, the next day after one of those nights where I eat a bit too much, I just tell myself I need to make up for it. So I might take an extra-long hike, or if we have softball conditioning, I'll just push myself a little harder, even if I'm feeling a bit tired. And I'll usually just skip breakfast and lunch that day, too. \n\nIt's just to keep things balanced, you know? I think a lot of people push themselves a little harder after a big dinner, don't they? It's just being responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to balance out your eating are motivated by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"fear\" is the right word. It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're not eating or exercising?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I stay pretty busy during the day. Between meeting with clients and writing up policies, I don't really have the time to just sit around and dwell on my figure, you know? \n\nIt really only crosses my mind when I'm getting dressed in the morning, or like I said, if I'm trying to balance things out after a heavy meal. But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? Being able to just focus on my work and not obsess over it all day?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your self-worth is tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say my whole self-worth is tied to it. That makes it sound so shallow, and I really try not to be a shallow person. I know I have value in other ways. \n\nBut... I guess, like I was saying earlier, it does affect how I feel about myself in the moment. Like, if my waistband feels tight before a client luncheon or a leadership meeting, I start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I start worrying that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I've still been doing my job well and my clients trust me. \n\nIt just... it makes me feel less confident, I suppose. Less put-together. But that's just because I want to present myself well professionally, you know? It's important to look the part when you're an agent, right? I'm sure you understand that, being a doctor and having to look professional yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your eating are driven by a concern about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I think... well, we talked about this a little bit just a moment ago, didn't we? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... wow, I've never really thought about it like that. I'm... I'm honestly not sure? 

I'd like to think I would still want to eat right and take care of my body, even if weight wasn't a factor at all. It's not *just* about the scale, you know? It's also about feeling energetic for my clients and not feeling all sluggish and heavy if I happen to overdo it at a dinner. 

So, I think I would still try to be mindful and keep things in check... just because it feels better to be healthy and active. Does that make sense? I just think it's always good practice to have some kind of routine and moderation in your life, don't you agree?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Gosh, that's... that's quite a hypothetical. If I knew I couldn't gain a single pound no matter what I ate? 

I honestly don't know. It's hard to even wrap my head around a scenario like that. But... I'd like to think I would still try to eat a balanced diet. Because even if weight wasn't an issue at all, health still is, right? At fifty, you have to start thinking about things like your energy levels and your digestion, not just your waistline. 

So I think I would still try to control what I eat to some degree, just to be a responsible adult. I mean, eating whatever you want all the time wouldn't be very good for you internally, would it? I'm sure you tell your patients that all the time. It's just about taking good care of yourself and being sensible, don't you think?

So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're not eating or exercising?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I stay pretty busy during the day. Between meeting with clients and writing up policies, I don't really have the time to just sit around and dwell on my figure, you know? \n\nIt really only crosses my mind when I'm getting dressed in the morning, or like I said, if I'm trying to balance things out after a heavy meal. But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? Well, um... I suppose they might change a little bit? I mean, I try not to be superficial or overly focused on my appearance, but... well, in my line of work, presentation does matter.

Like, I'm a grown woman, I should be able to just have one bowl of cereal and stop, you know? \n\nIt's just a bit... frustrating, I guess. I try so hard to be good during the day, so when I slip up like that, I just feel a little disappointed in myself. But I don't want to make it sound like some big, dramatic thing. It's just a bad habit I've gotten into, really. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... guilty might be a slightly strong word for it? I mean, I definitely don't feel proud, but I wouldn't say I'm agonizing over it or anything. \n\nIt's more just a little twinge of, \"Oh, I shouldn't have done that,\" and then I just try to move on and promise myself I'll be better the next day. I really try not to dwell on it too much, because I know everyone overindulges sometimes, right? I just... I try to keep a positive attitude about it. Is that... is that a normal way to feel about it?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever do anything to try to make up for eating more than you meant to, like exercising extra or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um, well, yes, I suppose I do try to balance things out a little bit? Just to keep things even, you know? \n\nLike, the next day, if I've had one of those nights, I'll just tell myself I'm making up for it. So I might, um, just skip breakfast and lunch. Or, you know, I like to stay active, so I might take an extra-long hike, or maybe push myself a little harder at softball conditioning... even if I'm feeling a bit tired. \n\nIt just seems like a healthy way to handle it, right? Just a little extra effort to get back on track. I hope that's the right approach? I try really hard to be responsible about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no. No, I would never do anything like that. That sounds... well, it sounds a bit extreme, doesn't it? \n\nI know some people struggle with that sort of thing, and my heart goes out to them, but I just try to stick to the exercise and watching what I eat the next day. I'd never want to do anything that could actually harm my body, you know? I really try to be sensible about my health. That's the right way to look at it, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I'm really thinking about it, for the past few months or so, it's been happening most weeks? Usually just, you know, one or two nights a week where I end up eating a bit more than I planned. \n\nAnd then, like I said, the next day I just try to be really good about it. I'll skip a meal or two, or maybe just stay out on the trail a little longer to make up for it. It's really not every single day or anything out of control. Just a couple of times a week to keep things balanced. That's... that's not too unusual, is it? I feel like a lot of women my age probably do the same thing to stay in shape, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I think my weight has stayed pretty steady, actually. I mean, maybe a pound or two up or down, just normal fluctuation, but nothing significant at all. \n\nI really try to keep on top of it, you know, with the extra walking and just being careful the next day. So it stays pretty much the exact same. I figure as long as my weight isn't jumping all over the place, I'm managing things okay... right? That's usually a good sign?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, afraid? I don't know if I'd use the word afraid. That sounds a little... intense, maybe? \n\nI mean, I definitely prefer not to gain weight, but I think that's just normal, right? Working in sales—I sell insurance, you know—you really want to look presentable and professional for your clients. I just like to maintain where I am so my clothes fit nicely and I feel healthy. I think everyone worries about their waistline a little bit as they get older... wouldn't you say that's pretty standard?\n\nDoctor: How often do you skip meals the day after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it kind of goes hand in hand with those nights I mentioned earlier. So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? Like, a little course correction just once or twice a week?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your exercise routine to be excessive or more than what a typical person your age would do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say it's *excessive*. That sounds like I'm, you know, out of control or something. \n\nI just... like I said, the next day after one of those nights where I eat a bit too much, I just tell myself I need to make up for it. So I might take an extra-long hike, or if we have softball conditioning, I'll just push myself a little harder, even if I'm feeling a bit tired. And I'll usually just skip breakfast and lunch that day, too. \n\nIt's just to keep things balanced, you know? I think a lot of people push themselves a little harder after a big dinner, don't they? It's just being responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to balance out your eating are motivated by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"fear\" is the right word. It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're not eating or exercising?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I stay pretty busy during the day. Between meeting with clients and writing up policies, I don't really have the time to just sit around and dwell on my figure, you know? \n\nIt really only crosses my mind when I'm getting dressed in the morning, or like I said, if I'm trying to balance things out after a heavy meal. But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? Being able to just focus on my work and not obsess over it all day?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your self-worth is tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say my whole self-worth is tied to it. That makes it sound so shallow, and I really try not to be a shallow person. I know I have value in other ways. \n\nBut... I guess, like I was saying earlier, it does affect how I feel about myself in the moment. Like, if my waistband feels tight before a client luncheon or a leadership meeting, I start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I start worrying that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I've still been doing my job well and my clients trust me. \n\nIt just... it makes me feel less confident, I suppose. Less put-together. But that's just because I want to present myself well professionally, you know? It's important to look the part when you're an agent, right? I'm sure you understand that, being a doctor and having to look professional yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your eating are driven by a concern about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I think... well, we talked about this a little bit just a moment ago, didn't we? It's really just about wanting to feel put together and professional. \n\nI wouldn't call it a \"concern\" about gaining weight, exactly. That sounds so... drastic. It's more just, um, wanting to maintain where I'm at. I just want my clothes to fit comfortably so I don't have to worry about looking unkempt when I'm meeting with clients. \n\nI just try to be mindful and keep things balanced. That's a healthy way to approach it, isn't it? Just trying to maintain a professional appearance?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to balance out your eating if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, I've never really thought about it like that. I'm... I'm honestly not sure? \n\nI'd like to think I would still want to eat right and take care of my body, even if weight wasn't a factor at all. It's not *just* about the scale, you know? It's also about feeling energetic for my clients and not feeling all sluggish and heavy if I happen to overdo it at a dinner. \n\nSo, I think I would still try to be mindful and keep things in check... just because it feels better to be healthy and active. Does that make sense? I just think it's always good practice to have some kind of routine and moderation in your life, don't you agree?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your eating if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: Gosh, that's... that's quite a hypothetical. If I knew I couldn't gain a single pound no matter what I ate? \n\nI honestly don't know. It's hard to even wrap my head around a scenario like that. But... I'd like to think I would still try to eat a balanced diet. Because even if weight wasn't an issue at all, health still is, right? At fifty, you have to start thinking about things like your energy levels and your digestion, not just your waistline. \n\nSo I think I would still try to control what I eat to some degree, just to be a responsible adult. I mean, eating whatever you want all the time wouldn't be very good for you internally, would it? I'm sure you tell your patients that all the time. It's just about taking good care of yourself and being sensible, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in control of what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I do like to have some structure. \"Control\" sounds a bit intense, doesn't it? It makes it sound like I'm obsessing over every little crumb, which I'm really not. \n\nI just like to have a general plan for the day, you know? Like making sure I have a sensible lunch so I'm not starving by the time I get home. It just helps me feel a bit more organized, especially when work gets chaotic with all the different policies and client calls. \n\nI think most successful people try to have some discipline in their daily routines, right? It's just a way of staying focused and on track. That's a pretty normal way to handle things, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you weren't worried about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose they might change a little bit? I mean, I try not to be superficial or overly focused on my appearance, but... well, in my line of work, presentation does matter. \n\nLike, if I'm getting dressed for a client luncheon or a big leadership meeting, and my waistband feels a little tight... I don't know, I just start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I worry that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I'm perfectly good at my job and I've been doing it a long time. It just shakes my confidence a bit, you know? \n\nSo, I guess if I didn't care at all about how my body looked in my clothes, maybe I wouldn't be as careful about what I eat beforehand. But wanting to look professional and put-together for work... that's a pretty normal thing to care about, isn't it? I think most women in business feel that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose so. I mean, in insurance, you're selling trust as much as you're selling a policy, right? If you don't look sharp and capable, people might second-guess whether you can handle their accounts. 

So, sure, I try to be mindful of what I eat so I can maintain a professional appearance. I wouldn't say it completely dictates my life or anything dramatic like that. It's more just... being aware, I guess? Like, I might skip dessert at a business dinner so I don't feel sluggish or bloated the next morning when I have to put on a suit. 

I think anyone who wants to succeed in their career makes little adjustments like that, don't they? It's just part of being a dedicated professional... I'm sure you understand what I mean, having to present yourself well for your patients every day.

But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? That's the right way to look at it, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating more than you meant to happen?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess if I'm really thinking about it, for the past few months or so, it's been happening most weeks? Usually just, you know, one or two nights a week where I end up eating a bit more than I planned. \n\nAnd then, like I said, the next day I just try to be really good about it. I'll skip a meal or two, or maybe just stay out on the trail a little longer to make up for it. It's really not every single day or anything out of control. Just a couple of times a week to keep things balanced. That's... that's not too unusual, is it? I feel like a lot of women my age probably do the same thing to stay in shape, right?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I think my weight has stayed pretty steady, actually. I mean, maybe a pound or two up or down, just normal fluctuation, but nothing significant at all. \n\nI really try to keep on top of it, you know, with the extra walking and just being careful the next day. So it stays pretty much the exact same. I figure as long as my weight isn't jumping all over the place, I'm managing things okay... right? That's usually a good sign?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, afraid? I don't know if I'd use the word afraid. That sounds a little... intense, maybe? \n\nI mean, I definitely prefer not to gain weight, but I think that's just normal, right? Working in sales—I sell insurance, you know—you really want to look presentable and professional for your clients. I just like to maintain where I am so my clothes fit nicely and I feel healthy. I think everyone worries about their waistline a little bit as they get older... wouldn't you say that's pretty standard?\n\nDoctor: How often do you skip meals the day after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it kind of goes hand in hand with those nights I mentioned earlier. So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? Like, a little course correction just once or twice a week?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your exercise routine to be excessive or more than what a typical person your age would do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say it's *excessive*. That sounds like I'm, you know, out of control or something. \n\nI just... like I said, the next day after one of those nights where I eat a bit too much, I just tell myself I need to make up for it. So I might take an extra-long hike, or if we have softball conditioning, I'll just push myself a little harder, even if I'm feeling a bit tired. And I'll usually just skip breakfast and lunch that day, too. \n\nIt's just to keep things balanced, you know? I think a lot of people push themselves a little harder after a big dinner, don't they? It's just being responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to balance out your eating are motivated by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"fear\" is the right word. It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're not eating or exercising?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I stay pretty busy during the day. Between meeting with clients and writing up policies, I don't really have the time to just sit around and dwell on my figure, you know? \n\nIt really only crosses my mind when I'm getting dressed in the morning, or like I said, if I'm trying to balance things out after a heavy meal. But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? Being able to just focus on my work and not obsess over it all day?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your self-worth is tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say my whole self-worth is tied to it. That makes it sound so shallow, and I really try not to be a shallow person. I know I have value in other ways. \n\nBut... I guess, like I was saying earlier, it does affect how I feel about myself in the moment. Like, if my waistband feels tight before a client luncheon or a leadership meeting, I start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I start worrying that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I've still been doing my job well and my clients trust me. \n\nIt just... it makes me feel less confident, I suppose. Less put-together. But that's just because I want to present myself well professionally, you know? It's important to look the part when you're an agent, right? I'm sure you understand that, being a doctor and having to look professional yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your eating are driven by a concern about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I think... well, we talked about this a little bit just a moment ago, didn't we? It's more just, um, wanting to maintain where I'm at. I just want my clothes to fit comfortably so I don't have to worry about looking unkempt when I'm meeting with clients. \n\nI just try to be mindful and keep things balanced. That's a healthy way to approach it, isn't it? I think my weight has stayed pretty steady, actually. I mean, maybe a pound or two up or down, just normal fluctuation, but nothing significant at all. \n\nI really try to keep on top of it, you know, with the extra walking and just being careful the next day. So it stays pretty much the exact same. I figure as long as my weight isn't jumping all over the place, I'm managing things okay... right? That's usually a good sign?\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Um, afraid? I don't know if I'd use the word afraid. That sounds a little... intense, maybe? \n\nI mean, I definitely prefer not to gain weight, but I think that's just normal, right? Working in sales—I sell insurance, you know—you really want to look presentable and professional for your clients. I just like to maintain where I am so my clothes fit nicely and I feel healthy. I think everyone worries about their waistline a little bit as they get older... wouldn't you say that's pretty standard?\n\nDoctor: How often do you skip meals the day after eating more than you meant to?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I guess it kind of goes hand in hand with those nights I mentioned earlier. So, for the past few months, it's been about once or twice a week. \n\nIf I have one of those evenings where I end up eating a bit more than I intended, then the very next day is when I'll skip a meal, or maybe just go for a much longer walk. It's really just a direct response to those specific nights, you know? Just trying to balance it out right away so it doesn't become a problem. That makes sense, right? Like, a little course correction just once or twice a week?\n\nDoctor: Do you consider your exercise routine to be excessive or more than what a typical person your age would do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I wouldn't say it's *excessive*. That sounds like I'm, you know, out of control or something. \n\nI just... like I said, the next day after one of those nights where I eat a bit too much, I just tell myself I need to make up for it. So I might take an extra-long hike, or if we have softball conditioning, I'll just push myself a little harder, even if I'm feeling a bit tired. And I'll usually just skip breakfast and lunch that day, too. \n\nIt's just to keep things balanced, you know? I think a lot of people push themselves a little harder after a big dinner, don't they? It's just being responsible, right?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to balance out your eating are motivated by a fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't know if \"fear\" is the right word. It's more about, um, just wanting to feel put together? \n\nLike, if I'm getting ready for a client luncheon, or maybe one of our leadership meetings at the agency, and my waistband feels just a little bit tight... I start feeling really self-conscious. I start worrying that I look sloppy, you know? And in my line of work, if you don't look sharp, people might not take you as seriously. \n\nI mean, I know I'm still doing my job well, and I know my clients trust me, but... it just shakes my confidence a little if my clothes aren't fitting perfectly. So I just try to keep everything in check so I don't have to worry about that. That's a pretty normal thing to care about when you work with the public, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight or body shape when you're not eating or exercising?\n\nPatient: Well, um... not really? I mean, I stay pretty busy during the day. Between meeting with clients and writing up policies, I don't really have the time to just sit around and dwell on my figure, you know? \n\nIt really only crosses my mind when I'm getting dressed in the morning, or like I said, if I'm trying to balance things out after a heavy meal. But otherwise, I'm just focused on my job and my day-to-day life. I don't let it consume my thoughts or anything. That's a good thing, right? Being able to just focus on my work and not obsess over it all day?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your self-worth is tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say my whole self-worth is tied to it. That makes it sound so shallow, and I really try not to be a shallow person. I know I have value in other ways. \n\nBut... I guess, like I was saying earlier, it does affect how I feel about myself in the moment. Like, if my waistband feels tight before a client luncheon or a leadership meeting, I start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I start worrying that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I've still been doing my job well and my clients trust me. \n\nIt just... it makes me feel less confident, I suppose. Less put-together. But that's just because I want to present myself well professionally, you know? It's important to look the part when you're an agent, right? I'm sure you understand that, being a doctor and having to look professional yourself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your efforts to control your eating are driven by a concern about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I think... well, we talked about this a little bit just a moment ago, didn't we? It's really just about wanting to feel put together and professional. \n\nI wouldn't call it a \"concern\" about gaining weight, exactly. That sounds so... drastic. It's more just, um, wanting to maintain where I'm at. I just want my clothes to fit comfortably so I don't have to worry about looking unkempt when I'm meeting with clients. \n\nI just try to be mindful and keep things balanced. That's a healthy way to approach it, isn't it? Just trying to maintain a professional appearance?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to balance out your eating if you didn't worry about gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, I've never really thought about it like that. I'm... I'm honestly not sure? \n\nI'd like to think I would still want to eat right and take care of my body, even if weight wasn't a factor at all. It's not *just* about the scale, you know? It's also about feeling energetic for my clients and not feeling all sluggish and heavy if I happen to overdo it at a dinner. \n\nSo, I think I would still try to be mindful and keep things in check... just because it feels better to be healthy and active. Does that make sense? I just think it's always good practice to have some kind of routine and moderation in your life, don't you agree?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your eating if you knew you couldn't gain weight no matter what you ate?\n\nPatient: Gosh, that's... that's quite a hypothetical. If I knew I couldn't gain a single pound no matter what I ate? \n\nI honestly don't know. It's hard to even wrap my head around a scenario like that. But... I'd like to think I would still try to eat a balanced diet. Because even if weight wasn't an issue at all, health still is, right? At fifty, you have to start thinking about things like your energy levels and your digestion, not just your waistline. \n\nSo I think I would still try to control what I eat to some degree, just to be a responsible adult. I mean, eating whatever you want all the time wouldn't be very good for you internally, would it? I'm sure you tell your patients that all the time. It's just about taking good care of yourself and being sensible, don't you think?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to be in control of what you eat?\n\nPatient: I mean... I guess I do like to have some structure. \"Control\" sounds a bit intense, doesn't it? It makes it sound like I'm obsessing over every little crumb, which I'm really not. \n\nI just like to have a general plan for the day, you know? Like making sure I have a sensible lunch so I'm not starving by the time I get home. It just helps me feel a bit more organized, especially when work gets chaotic with all the different policies and client calls. \n\nI think most successful people try to have some discipline in their daily routines, right? It's just a way of staying focused and on track. That's a pretty normal way to handle things, isn't it?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you weren't worried about how your body looks?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose they might change a little bit? I mean, I try not to be superficial or overly focused on my appearance, but... well, in my line of work, presentation does matter. \n\nLike, if I'm getting dressed for a client luncheon or a big leadership meeting, and my waistband feels a little tight... I don't know, I just start feeling like I look sloppy. And then I worry that people won't take me as seriously, even though I know I'm perfectly good at my job and I've been doing it a long time. It just shakes my confidence a bit, you know? \n\nSo, I guess if I didn't care at all about how my body looked in my clothes, maybe I wouldn't be as careful about what I eat beforehand. But wanting to look professional and put-together for work... that's a pretty normal thing to care about, isn't it? I think most women in business feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your eating habits are influenced by your professional life and the need to present yourself in a certain way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose so. I mean, in insurance, you're selling trust as much as you're selling a policy, right? If you don't look sharp and capable, people might second-guess whether you can handle their accounts. \n\nSo, sure, I try to be mindful of what I eat so I can maintain a professional appearance. I wouldn't say it completely dictates my life or anything dramatic like that. It's more just... being aware, I guess? Like, I might skip dessert at a business dinner so I don't feel sluggish or bloated the next morning when I have to put on a suit. \n\nI think anyone who wants to succeed in their career makes little adjustments like that, don't they? It's just part of being a dedicated professional... I'm sure you understand what I mean, having to present yourself well for your patients every day.\n\nDoctor: Would you still feel the need to control your eating if your professional appearance wasn't a concern?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... wow, I've never really thought about that. I guess... well, I'm not entirely sure. It's hard to separate the two, really. \n\nI suppose if I retired tomorrow and didn't have to worry about client meetings, maybe I wouldn't be quite as... careful? But I think even then, I'd still want to look nice and feel healthy, you know? I don't think I have this overwhelming need to control everything like it sounds. It's really just about taking care of myself. \n\nI think most women want to take care of themselves and feel good in their own skin, whether they're working or not... don't they? Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
